LADIES' LETTER.

MT DEAR NELLIE'

There are some exceedingly pretty girl coats coming to the front now. One I saw was made in fine blue cloth, with dainty collar of nainsook and guipure with a hat of guipure over white chiffon, white bows, and loops of crinoline straw. Then you may see how you can secure the smart- est little open-fronted coat of black or blue serge, the tapering revers and little rounded basque edged with black and white braid, its name, by the way, being the " Milan" ; while the "Dore," shows the latest development of the bolero coat, cut short to the waist at the back and sides, but made with longer fronts, its material black or tan box cloth, with collar and revers of morid, a braided trimming and a silken lining.

As smart a spring wrap as the ireart of woman could desire, is of a type that will be exbrennely fashionable during the next few months. It is made of rich black poult de soie, edged all round with fine jet gimp, and having big revers of ivory satin, overlaid with black sequin embroidered net the edges softened with quillings of chiffon. The becoming Medici collar is lined with cream Mech- lin lace, and a voluminous cravat of the same fine lace provides a very pretty finish. The coat hooks invisibly at the left side, where .it is adorned with two large cut glass buttons. Another most excellent coat is the box cloth "Doris," where an elaborate arrangement of stitched strappings is responsible for the adorn- ment of it tight-fitting simplicity. Then the " Perfection" lives up to its name by being the smartest little coat possible of black, tan, or grey box cloth, the scalloped Medici collar, the prettily- rounded fronts and tiny basque outlined with a novel design of stitching, while the revers are faced with white moird—a fact which you can make decoratively obvious when you so desire, while on other occasions you can quite alter the character of your coat by closing it up trimly and tightly, and fastening it over in double-breasted fashion, but of these, it only.now remains for you to choose your favourite.

There are some very smart serge coats and skirt costumes. If you are in search of a promenade or afternoon gown, there is the " Adele " to show the effectiveness of black and white check com- bined with. stitched strappings of blank satin, an underskirt of the same fablic, and on the bodice a tucked collar and yoke of pale blue . silk, with a scarf and sailor's knot of lace. Each coat and gown is provided with a companion in the way of a hat or toque; and, is unions of this kind are proverbially happy and successful, you will be wise nob to interfere with them.

In regard to millinery there is any amount of varieties, for the show of millinery this spring is even more attractive than usual. Many of these are shown in the fashionable papers, with full description of the trimmings.

One I have seen is a hat of fancy cream-coloured straw, with a fancy Tuscan straw threaded in the plait. This is trimmed all round the crown with black ostrich tips, and twists of black tulle are caught with a handsome silver buckle set with 5 paste and emeralds. There are pipings of black velvet under the brim, and a wreath of exquisitely natural pink roses rests on the hair in front. Then there is a boat-shaped hat of fancy straw in a pale shade of fawn, trimmed with silk of two colours, pluinbago-blue and a dead shade of red, which make.; a daring but very effective cotabina- tion of colour. Long quills of a natural' shade appear above the folds of silk, and these are painted in large white designs. Under the brim on either aide are pink and white roses. Your loving JENNIE.

CURTLY SAuon —Melt three tablespoonfuls of butter, add three tablespoonfuls of flour mixed with one teaspoonful of curry powder and half a teaspoonful of salt. Pour on gradmally two cup- fuls of scalded milk. Tha ambasit ol curry used in this recipe will not be objected to by moss persons unless the flavour is very distasteful to them.

THE EXAMINER FRWAY, APRIL 21, 1899.

"WOMEN'S CHAT."

Princess Henry of. Battenberg, who has lately cele- brated the forty-second anniversary of her birthday, seems to have recovered to a cons derable extent, her bright, cheery manner, which has been missed by many since the death of Prince Henry three years ago. Her Royal Highness still dons mourning, but it is of much slighter character than hitherto, and really suits the Princess better than those colours she was wont to in- dulge in. Prince Alexander, her eldest son is growing quite tall, ar dit is pretty to see his air of protection when with his mother. He is a quiet thoughtful laddie, and in disposition resembles his father. The Queen is, and 'always has been a great advoCkte for the observance of birthdays. In her Majiisty's " Birthday Book "the names of all her relatives are re- corded, and as each anniversary comes round,the Queen writer congratulations with her own hand. To her chil- dren and grandchildren a gift specially, and thought- fully eelected,is always despatched by special messenger on the anniversary of their natal day. By her daughters and daughters-in-law, the Queen is invariably addressed as " Mamma." This word has become 83 unfashionable of late years ia all but the humblest classes of society, that one at first feels sur- prised to hear the ,Princesses use it to their own mother. But we have only to remember that in the days when cur Queen, as Princess Victoria, had her own beloved mother with her, the highest classes in the land used the word " Mamma " in addressing the maternal parent. The Queen is strongly conservative in all such matters. Lord and Lsdy Iveagb, who had the honour of enter- taining the Duke and Duchess of York during their re- cent visit to Ireland, are enormously rich. Beside their Dublin house, tt,ey have a mansion in Grosvenor Place. London, filled with the most valuable collecti.ns of Roinneys in existence, and they own Elvedon, where the late Maharajah Dhuleep Singh used to live. The ball- room in th, it Dublin house is said to have coat £30,000. The walls are panelled out in pilasters of al.baster in double columns, dividing high pa:;.els of Algerian onyx, in frames of Carrara marble. At either end is a balcony of aluminium, looking like silver, and the huge massive fender before the fireplace is ot the same metal. • The wonderful oak floor is delightfully " springy," and the electric light so arranged • that the room receives only reflected light. The batigings are of.rose coloured silk appliqUe, with velvet of darker hue and richly embroi- dered.

So soon as the business of hou3e cleaning is got through, the numerous items of" decoration" should be taken in band. For instance, lamp audcandle shades,that have looked very passable during the dark winter even- ings now appear dingy and faded in the bright spring sunlight, sofa cushioes bear a crumpled aspect, a new. bow is re eded here, a new card or flower is neceesary there, in fact at this seae,on there are a hundred und one little details that need attention, if we would have Our homes present that bright well-cared-for look which is the aspiration of all model housekeepers. To begin with the lamps. All burners should te placed in soda and water (one o•ince to every quart of water) and boiled for ten minutes. New wicks should be soaked in strong vinegar and thoroughly dried before placing in thescleaned buiners,and the lamp chimneys cleaned with &cloth dipped in alcohol. If the stock of chimneys is getting low, this should be replenished, and boiled t efore storing: The ceitnney should be placed in a saucepan of cold water, which should gradually be brought to toiling point, the saucepan being tben drawn aside. and 'he water allowed to become quite cold before the chimneys are removed. Silk shades are the most desir- able of course, but where something lass expensive is desired, crinkled paper with delicate blossoms printed on it is the best substitute. For reading, a green or rose pink shade is best, but for the dinner table or drawing room, red or yellow are becoming colours. One of the newest and prettiest ways of decorating a dinner table is to hang a basket from the ring in the ceiling to which the now almost obsolete gas chandslier used to be attached. From this ba,ket long trails of smilax, ivy, (brown and green) with each leaf polished to a high perfection of glossiness should depend. Any rusty leaves must be carefully cut away, or the erect will be spoiled. A few fashionable American women are now disp, rising with a tablecloth altogether. A plush or ' satin centre decorates the middle of the festive beard, and a mat of the same material is put to each guest for the plates. On the highly polished surface of the table are arranged the silver and china dess‘rt dishes, silver lamps or candle sticks, and flower bowls. The idea may be novel, but is scarcely likely to find favour in our eyes. The new menus and holders are in various fantastic designs, but the" poster" is still found among them, the list of dishes being deposed upon it in a manner rather suegesti re of the contents of a magazine, than a dainty fare for the mere body. Above it the word "Menu "straggles in the brightest colours, with each letter apparently endeavouring to stride off the page in a different direction. For special occasions' menu cards ate usually specially designed and printed. THE SEAFARERS :-

A MODERN ROMANCE.

BY JOHN BLOUNDELLE-BURTON, Author of "Fortune's my Foe," "The Scourge of God," "Across the Salt Seas," "The Clash of Arms," "In the Day of Adversity," "A Gen- tleman Adventurer," "His Own Enemy," "Denounced," eirc.

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS: Arabella Waldron, a young girl, is to sail for India in a ship belonging to her uncle, Captain Pooley, in order td be married at Bombay. But previously the reader is to be told of her courtship. A year before, the Captain had taken her to the theatre, where she had met Steplnn Charke, a young friend of her uncle, who is smitten by her.

===== 0002601_18990421_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAPTER 111.

"LET THOSE LOVE NOW WIIO NEVER LOVED BEFORE.. The "captain " led the way into the five stalls he had booked, followed, of course, by the elder ladies, and, as Stephen Charke naturally went last, it fell out that Bella and he sat by each other. And between the acts, the intervals of which were quite long enough .for _sustained conversation to take place, the girl had time to find held interest in him, as well as her surprise, considerably increased. She had perused a few novels dealing with the sea in. her time, and, in these, the mates of ships of whom she had read had more or less served to con- firm her opinion, already formed from real life. But, when Charke began to talk to her about the actual source from which; the ,play they were witnessing was drawn, she acknowledged to herself that, somehow she must have conceived a wrong impression of those seafarers. Certainly he, she , thought, gould not be one of the creatures who cursed and abused the men if they objected to their food, and threatened next to put them in irons; nor did she believe such individuals would have been depicted as knowing much about the Revolu- tion in France and the vulgar triumphs of Napoleon. Then, during the next interval, he approached the subject of the forthcoming festivities at Ports- mouth, to which Bella's uncle had told him he was going to take his relatives, and from that he glided off into the statement that he himself would be there. "I am going down next Monday," he said, "to see one or two of my old messmates of the Bacchus —in which I served for a year in the Channel Squadron—and I fancy 1 shall be ja at most of the functions. Have you ever been to a naval ball ? " Bella told him she never had been to one, her mother's intimacy with the Service having entirely ceased since Captain Waldron's death, and he then • proceeded to give her an account of what these delightful functions were like. Indeed, so vividly did he pourtray them that Bella almost wished they were going on that very night to take part in-one. When the play was over she—who acknow- ledged to herself that which probably no power on earth would have induced her to acknowledge so soon to anyone else, namely, that Stephen Charke was an agreeable, if not a fascinating, companion—in company with the others prepared to return to West Kensington, bidding good-bye to him in the vestibule of the theatre. "Where 'are you staying?" Mrs. Waldron asked him, as they stood on the steps waiting for their cab to make its appearance in turn. "And are you in London for any time ? " Charke mentioned the name of a West Epd caravanserai at which he had a room, ,as Fiis abode for the next day or so, and by doing so he administered one more shock of surprise to the girl' standing hooded and muffled by his side. For again, in her ignorance, or, perhaps, owing to heir readieg of nautical 'novels, she had always imagined the officers of merchant vessels as living somewhere in the purlieus of Ratcliff Highway when ashore, and rarely penetrating further West than the City itself. It seemed, however, flint either she had formed a totally erroneous impression of such people, dr those sources of information must be wronc, if all were really like Mr. Charke. But then, suddenly, here occurred to her mind the fact that her uncledriad said this young officer NNiS in possession of sonic few thousand pounds of his own—and .that, probably, wald explain why he was living in a comfortable manner when he Was ashore. "I am at home to my friends ervery other Friday," Mrs. Waldron said, as now the cab had got to the portico, and a man was bawling out "Mrs. POoley's carriage" at the top of his voice— which announcement, nevertheless, served the purpose required—"and the day after to-morrow happens to be one of those Fridays. If you clue to call— though I know gentlemen despise such things—we shall be glad to see you." "I do not despise them," Charke answered, "and I shall be dt lighted to come." Then he bade them all good-night, saw the cab off, and strolled down to his betel. In his inner- ntost heart Charke did despise such things as "at homes," or "tea fights," as he termed them con-

temptuously to himself, yet, in, common with a great many other mess, he was willing enough to go where there was any attraction strong enough to draw him. - And he told himself that there was an attraction such as he had never been subjected to before. "What a lovely girl," he thought to himself; "what eyes and hair—and a nice girl, too! Now I begin to understand why other men curse the sea, and say they would rather earn their living on shore driving 'busses than follow our calling. Also, why they nail up photographs in their cabins and watch every chance of getting mails off from the shore. I suppose I should have understood it earlier if I had ever met a girl like this." He did call on the following Friday, after having passed the inter' fling two days in wandering about London; going to a race meeting one day and a cricket match at the Oval the next; in trying a dinner at one foreign restaurant on the Wednesday, and another at a second foreign restaurant on Thursday: .but all the time he felt restless and unsettled, and wished that four o'clock on Friday was, at hand.. "This won't do," he said to himself, before, the cricket match on Thursday was half over, and while he sat baking in the sun that streamed down on to the Oval—which disturbed him not at all, and had no power to make him any browner—"this won't do., I must go to sea at once. By the time I have seen that girl again I shall be head over ears in love with her. And the interest on £4,000 in India Stock—by Jove! it isn't quite £4,000 since I've been loafing about on shore !—and a chief officer's pay won't keep a wife. Not such a wife as she would be, anyway." He did not. know it—or, perhaps, he did know it and would not acknowledge it to himself—but he was very nearly head over ears in love with Bella Waldron already. And he had only seen her once —been by her side at a theatre for three hours— with three intervals of ten minutes ir. which to talk to her! Yet the girl's beauty, her gentle innocence, above all, that trusting confidence with-which she seemed to look out upon all that was passing before her, and to regard the world as what it appeared to he and to take it at its own valuation, had captured him. Also, he should have known—he must have known—that when a man who has never thought much of the women he has Met heretofore, and has generally forgotten what their features were like by the next day, takes to lying awake for hours dreaming at last of one woman with whom he has •by chance' come into contact, he is as nearly in love with her as it is possible for him to be. So, at least, those report who have been in love, and so it has been told to the writer of this narrative! He made his way to Montmorency-road, West Kensingtob, exactly at four o'clock, and while he sat in the paetty drawing-room talking to Mrs. Waldron, who was alone at present—the appearance of Bella being promised by her mother in a few moments—he found himself wondering what the girl did witk her life here. He had seen bicycle in the passage as he was shewn upstairs, so he supposed she rbae chat, while there were some photographs of rather goad-looking men standing about on tha semi-grand and on the plush-covered thantelshelf—which made him feel horribly annoyed, until Mrs. Waldron, seeing his glance fixed on them, informed him that they were mostly cousins who were out of the country, while one or two of them happened to have succumbed to various climatic disorders abroad, for' which he did not seem to feel as sorry as he supposed he ought to do. Then Bella came in, looking radiantly beauti- ful in an early summer dress (a description of which masculine ignorance renders impossible), and Stephen Charke was happy for ten minutes. For they all talked of the impending fttes at Ports- mouth in honour of the foreign fleet, and Charke found himself in an Elysium when Bella promised

nim—without the slightest selr-conseiousnesS 02 false shame—that she would undoubtedly hay. some dances reserved for him. Yet soon other callers came in, and Stephen Charke found himself deprived of the pleasure of further conversation with Bella. An elderly dowager claimed her attention, and a middle-aged lady—of, as he considered, menacing aspect— regaled him with the evil doings of her domestic servants, a subject of abcut as much interest to this wanderer of the seas as that of embroidery or tatting would have been. An Irish Doctor of Divinity also disturbed his meditations on Bella's beauty by telling funny stories, the point of which he had forgotten until he refreshed his memory by reference to a little notebook in which he had got them all written down' while a young militia subaltern who htd failed for the Army—and seemed rather proud of it I—irritated him beyond endurance. Yet, even through this fatuous individual, there came something that was welcome to him, since he saw Bella regarding the youth with a look of scarcely veiled contempt, and he longed to tell the idiot that the only failure for which women have no pity. in this world is that of the intellect.

"Good-bye," he said to Bella, who accompanied him to the head of the stairs, after he had made his adieus to her mother. "Good-bye. Next week— Portsmouth—and—my dances." "I shall not forget," she said. After which he wandered off by devious and intri- cate ways, which reminded him of some ofthe narrow passages he knew of between the islands in the China seas, and so arrived at the District Railway. And all the time he was telling himself that he was a fool—an absolute fool. have fallen in love with a girl I have only seen twice," he meditated, as the train ran through the sulphurous regions underground and he endeavoured to protect his lungs by smoking cigarette after cigarette; "a girl who is not, and never can bq, anything to me. She will make a good match some day; she must make a good match—girls of her position and looks always do*; and a year or two hence I shall luff into some unearthly harbour abroad and run against Pooley, who will tell me that she has done Yet, all the same, he took comfort from re- membering that he had not seen anybody at Mrs. Waldron's "afternoon" who was likely to be the individual to carry her off. But, in spite of this soothing reflection, he braced himself to a stern resolution: determined_ that, as already in his life he had triumphed over other things, so he would triumph now. Triumph over this swift-flowering and still growing love; conquer it by absence from the object which inspired it; trample it down till there was nothing left of life in it.

"I have no money to keep a wife," he thought, as he walked up from Charing-cross Station to his hotel; "certainly no money to keep such a wife as she would be. And, even if I had, it is not likely that she would marry me, a common mate,' as I have heard ourselves called. Portsmouth shall end it," he concluded. "I'll have one good week there, and then to sea again on a long cruise. That ought to lo it! I'll go down to the docks to-marrow and see what's open. Wherefore, full of this determinate resolution to drive from out of him the frenzy which had taken possession of his heart and mind, he went to the hotel and read in his bedroom for an hour or so, during all of which time Bella Waldron's face was looking out at him from the pages of the Quarterly Review, and also until he went forth to try still another foreign restaurant. Yet she was there too, and her pure, innocent eyes were gazing at him across the imitation flowers and the red candle- shades in the middle of the table; and so she also was in the stalls of the Empire, _until he fell asleep in his seat. No was she absent from his mind during the long hours of the night.

CHAPTER IV. PORTENIOUTII EN FETE.

The lunch at, Whale Island was over, and there was a slight breathing space ere the garden-party, which followed it, began. Meanwhile, from South- sea pier, from down by the pontoon at the foot of the old Hard, and over from Gosport, picket-boats, steam pinnaces and launches—all belonging to her Majesty's ships lying out at Spithead—were coming fast, as well is shore boats and numerous other craft that blackened the waters. And they bore in .them a gaily-dressed crowd of men and women, the ladies being adorned in all those beauteous garments which they know so well how to assume on stich an occasion; while, among the gentlemen, frock-coats, tall hats and white waiStcoats, as well as full dress or "No. 1." uniform, were the .:order of the day. For all these ship's-boats, after putting off from the batile:thips and cruisers to which they belonged, had, by order of the Vice-Admiral com- manding the Channel Squadron, called at the above- named places to fetch off the visitors to the Whale Is] and festivities. Stephen Charke, in the uniform of the came in the picket-boat of the Bacchus, wherein he had been lunching with the ward-room .officers, and, as she went alongside of Southsea pier and afterwards at the Old Town pier, he had eagerly scannned the ladies who were waiting to be taken off. He was not, however, particularly disappointed or cast down at not seeing the one girl he was looking out for at either of them, since, in the continual departure of similar boats, and the running backwards and forwards of these craft between Whale Island and the landing-stages, it was of course hardly to be supposed that she would happen upon the particular boat in which he was. He saw her, however, directly he, with his companions, .had made their way to the lawn on which the wife of the Port Admiral was receiving her guests, and—in so .seeing 'her—he recognised instantly that he was not going to enjoy his afternoon as much as he had hoped to do. "Who's that ? " he asked of the Staff Commander of the Bacchns, with whom he happened to he walking at the moment. "I mean that Flag- Lieutenant talking to the young lady in the white dress? "

"That ? " replied his companion, regarding the young officer indicated. "Oh! that's Gilbert Bampfyld, flag-lieutenant to the Rear-Admiral. He's a ,*ood chap; I'll introduce you later. A lucky fellow, too. He's heir to his uncle, Lord :D'Aberr..on. He's all right," he concluded, in- consequently. "I- know the young lady," Stephen said. been at sea with her uncle "

"I've

"Good-looking," said the Staff-Commander, who was a single man. "Fine girl, too. I hope she's coming to the ball."

"Slit) is," Charke replied,- and then stood observing her companion from the little group of which they ncw formed part. Certainly the younc, officer was "all right," if good looks and a manly figure can entitle anyone to that qualification. He was undoubtedly hand- some, with the handsomeness . which women are stated (by authorities on such matters) to admire: his bright eyes and good complexion, as well as his dear-cut, regular features, leaving little else to desire. Also he was fairly tall, while, if anything were required to set off his appearance, it was fur- nished by his full-dress and his flag-lieutenant's aiguillettes. He was talking now in a:a animated way, as Charke" could see easily enough from where he stood by the refreshment tent; and it was not posslble for him to doubt that he was making him- self very interesting to Bella. For a moment Stephen stood hesitating as to whether he •should go up and present himself to the girl who had never been out of his thoughts since he said "good-bye" to her in West Kensing- ton ; then, while he still debated the matter in his mind, she saw him and smiled and nodded pleasantly, while she looked—as he thought—as though she expected he should come up to her. Which, of course, decided him. There was no affectation in the manner wherewith Bella greeted him; in truth, she was glad to see him and, honestly, as she did everything else, she said so.

"I have been lookiag for you for the last hull- hour," she remarked as be reached her side, "and wondering if you were coming or not " ; after which she introduced Stephen Charke and Gilbert Bampfyld to each other. Then, some other officers coining up at this moment, more introductions took place, while Bampfyld said that he must move off. "I have escaped from my Admiral for a few moments," he said, while he added with a laugh: "I am not quite sure, however, that he is not con- gratulating himself on having escaped from -me. I hope, Miss Waldron," he added, "that you have an invitation for the ball ? "

"Yes," Bella said; and fshl. smiled at Lien- tenant Bampfyld's request that he might not be forgotten on that occasion, though she did not say positively whether that calamity would occur or not. , Then, when he had moved on to join the distin- guished officer to whom it was his duty to be attached almost as tightly as a limpet to a rock, she said to Charke : "Come, now, and see mamma. , She is in the shade behind the tent, and she has found tin old friend of papa's." • - But it .was so evident that Mrs. Waldron was I thoroughly enjoying herself with that old friend,

who was-a •retirea post-captain (Stle was at tne moment of their arrival cngaged in reminiscences of the North American and West Indian stations), that soon they strolled away together, and, finding soon another shady seat, sat crown and passed an agreeable hour or so. Wherefore, as you may thus see, Stephen Charke did spend a happy afternoon, notwithstanding that first apparition of .the flag - lieutenant in converse with the girl who was now never out of his thoughts. Indeed, it would have been to him a perfect afternoon, had he not more than once seen Bampfyld (who again appeared to have escaped from his Admiral!). roaming about the place with a somewhat disconsolate, as well as penetrating, look upon his face, which look Charke construed into signifying that the other was seeking for the girl of whom he himself had now obtained temporary possession. However, evert so, he did not 'think it necessary to call Bella'e attention to the fact. But we must not tarry over these soft summer beguilements to which the old naval capital and all in her had given themselves up. There lie other matters before us—matters which, when they afterwards occurred, caused three people now partaking of these enjoyments to, perhaps, cast back their memories, and those memories that were not untinged by regret. Suffice it, therefore, that we hurry on and, o'erleaping another garden-party which took . place at the military commandant's, and an "at home" giver' on b-oard the foreign visitors' flagship, as-well as entertainments at which only the male sex were present, we come to the naval ball at the Town Hall. -

That was a great night, a night on which, if one may judge by subsequent events, many loving hearts were made happy.; on which, too, some saw the dawn of the first promise of future happiness—and one man, at least, was made unnappy. It was a great night! A night no more forgotten by three people in the days that followed it than was the garden-party which pre- ceded it by a day or so. • The First Lord of the Admiralty led off the quadrille with the wife of the Commander-in-Chief; the Prince who was in command of the foreign fleet dancing with the First Lord's wife; in that set round which the other guests formed a vast circle, there being the most prominent individuals then present in Portsmouth. And Bella, standing close by with her hand upon the arm of Stephen Charke while they waited for the first dance in which all the guests could Participate, felt that, at'last, -she knew what a ball was.

"It isn't quite like a State Ball," whispered Lieutenant Bampfyld to them as he passed by with his Admiral, he, being qualified to give such information in consequence of his duties as flag- lieutenant having often given him the opportunity of attending those great functions, "but it is much prettier." Then be disappeared for n time. "It could scarcely be prettier than it is," Bells said to Charke. "How has the room been made-so beautiful? " "The men of the Vernon have done-it all," Ver companion answered; "they are good at that sort of thing." As, indeed, they seemed to be, judging by the effect they had produced. Trophiek of arms, flags, devices, lifebuoys white as snow, with the names of vessels belonging both to the visitmfa and our- selves painted in gold upon them, decorated the- vast room; while from the dockyard had been, unearthed old armour and weapons, such as, in these present days, are now forgotten. 'Also the colour lent by various uniforms, naval, military, and marne, as well as by flowers and the bouquets carried by ladies, added to the brilliant scene-- while the sombre black of civilians helped to give a contrast to the bright hues. For civilians were not forgotten; Admiralty officials, private resi- dents, special correspondents—with a wary eye ott their watches, so that they might be able to rush over to the Post Office with their last messages for the great London and provincial papers—were all there. ••

"Come," said Stephen Charke, as the band of the Royal Marines struck up the first waltz, "come, Miss Waldron; it is our turn now." And for ten minutes he realised what happiness meant.

That he would have to resign her for the greater part of the evening he knew very well—her pro- gramme was already full I—his name appearing three times on it, and Lieutenant Bampfyld's also three times—yet he did so none the less willingly later for that knowledge. How could he? loved the girl with his whole heart and soul-- madly! "I shall love her always, until I die," he muttered to himself as he stood by, seeking ne other partner and watching her dancing now with the flag-lieutenant. Also he saw her dancing with the flag-lieutenant of the other Admiral—though that did not seem to him to be so disturbing s matter. "Till I die!" he repeated again ;then once more called himself a fool. His second dance with her arrived, and once more he was in his seventh heaven; for the moment he was again supremely happy. "I hope I may have the pleasure of taking you in to supper," he ahnost whispered in her ear as they paused for a moment for breath, and it seemed as if the light of his enjoyment—for that evening, at least—had been suddenly extinguished when she, raising those sweet, clear eyes to his, exclaimed: "Oh! I am so sorry. But I have promised Lieutenant Bampfyld that he do so., " . _ . -

For the remainder of the ball Charke did not let n' single dance pass by without taking part in it, 'he allowing his friends to introduce him right and left to any lady who happened to require a partner, though reserving, of course, the one for which he was engaged to Bella at what would be almost the end of the evening. In fact, as his friend the Staff Commander said, "he let himself go pretty considerably," and he so far exemplified that gentle- man's remark that he took in to supper one of the plainest of those middle-and ladies who happened to be gracing the ball with their presence. Yet this lady found nothing whatever to com- plain of to herself (to her friends she would have uttered no complaint of her cavalier, even though he had been as stupid as an owl and as dumb as a stone, she being a wary old campaigner), but, instead, thought him a charming companion. Perhaps, too, she had good reason to do so, since, from the moment he conducted her across the tero- porarily constructed bridge which led from the Town Hall proper to the supper tent erected in a vacant plot of ground, his conversation was full of smart sayings and pleasant, though occasionally sub-acid, remarks on things in general. Yet, naturally, it was impossible that she should knot', that the undoubtedly bright and piquant converse" tion with which he entertained her was partly pro• duced by his bitterness at seeing Gilbert Bampfyla and Bella enjoying themselves thoroughly at a table a deux close by where he and his partner were seated, and partly by his stoical determination to, "let things go." Also, too, by another deterrnina. tion at which he had arrived—namely, to go to ses' again at the very first moment he could find a ship.

THE OLD CANAL BRIDGE.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

THE NEW VICAR OF ST. PAUL'S, WARRINGTON The Rev. E. R. Tarbuck, the recently appointed vicar of St. Paul's, prsached his first sermon in the church on Sunday evening to a large congregation. At the conclu- sion he said he wished to he personal for a moment or , two. That was the first time he had spoken to them as I their vicar. The kind of Struggle in which they were engaged was of all strugeles the most noble, because as they struggled they raised those around them, they raised 'he sum ef the greatness of character, and the sum of the world's happiness. He was firmly determined that he would be loyal ta them in thought, word, and deed ; and he ivleed them to be loyal to him. He was a Lancashire man ; they were Lancashire people. They. could not, if they tried, refuse to give him a fair chance.. Thev,could'not refuse that favour. It was not a favour; it was jse tic'. They had done more than tb; t already and he had had signs that hia work amongst them would be highly appreciated, and that they would sanction anything they thought good that be started or continued in the parish. He wished to be made acquainted with all cases of sickness in the pariah. He was anxious to visit the sick •, to do so was the first call upon a clergy- man's time. The healthy could for a time get on well ; the sick neoded a clergyman, but he would remind them that it was no good going to a sick man who was non compos mentis. Let him know of sick cases at Once. The importance of attendance at communion was also impressed upon the conereeation, and allasion was made to the forthcoming confirmation by the bishop. He explained that he had arranged to hold classes for those who wished to be confirmed. He hoped the congrega- tion would pray for their new Vicar; they might be cer- tain no day would pass without the whole of the clergy praying for them—The rev. gentleman was on Tuee'lay instituted to the incumbency of St.Paul's,Warrington;et the Diocesan Office by the Lord Bishop. At Wycliffe Congregational Church On Sunday morn- ing, the Rev. J. Ynnee (pastor) baSed his disceurse on the text "1 gave them My Sabbath te be a sign between Me and them, that they' mieht know that I am the Lord that senet'firs them." Ezekiel xx. 12 v. In the course of his remarks he said that jest in proportion as reli- gious people secularisrd Sunday,they were unspiritualis- ing themselves. He confessed this seemed to him to be one of the greatest perils of their day. Some were Ray- ing that the present greatest peril of Eegland was that its Protestanism should be overcome by a re-crudescence of S;creelotalism and Popery. Without minimisirg the reality of that danger, thee might wetl ask whether the secularising of the Lord's Day was not even a greater national peril still ? To him, the saddest feature of this seculariestion of Sunday was that ,religious people se largely shared In it. It con'd not leS Matter of surpr'se that reilErfOUS people &amid make sectiler use of Sunday. They ceiold not wonder that they filled their roods with waggonettes and brakes and bicycles; that they crowded the railways or steamboats in the summer ; that they wanted the Sunday newspaper and clam- oured for the museums and picture , grilleoies. , and would like even the theatre also. But thapity was that ameng these irreligious misusers of Suneay were to be found such a growing number of religious misusers. Be it remembered it was the religious absentees that thinned their churches. He euggssted that thesdiwere matters that called for very serious thought in religious people. Many of their minds, no doubt, would recall an event that happened in ~London the previons Sunday that served to bring to the front the question of Sebhath observance. 'He referred to the issue, for the first time in the history of the leading London newsPaytrr, of a Sunday edition of the "Daily Telegraph" and the" Daily Mail." It was said that 700,000 of the one and 500,000 of the other were printed and se'd. If this venture on the part of these two papers was a atiecess, other dailies would follow suit, and then both in London and in the provinces, the newspaper would become as prominent on the Sunday as on the week day. He wanted to point out that this was a very, serious new departure, and one which, so far as .they could, Chrkian people should combine to defeat. It was an unblushing attempt to turn Sunday into a day of trade and commerce; to secularise the day which Enelish history and habit had hitherto supremely regarded RS having a sacred character and purpose. It was an attempt to make many work woven days instead of six. in order that a few might get the profits. It would compel mane a man to work on Sunday, who new cared for his day of reet,and even used it as a day of worship. A newsagent had pointed out that in London for every one engaged in producing newspapers fifty were engaged in their distribution. To the bulk of rewaaments their trade meant incessant toil, early and late, day by day. ,ind now the sixth day was to he followed by a seventh., This was a position of cemmercial tvrannr,, and should' be risented and resisted. RUGBY FOOTBALL.

A PLUCKY CURA-TH. On Saturday aoout 1:10J11,8 horse attached to a cart belonging to :Mr. Renshaw, of Appleton, took fright and bolted in Bridge-stree, da,hing at a high rate of speed towards Bridge-foot, when the Rev. T. W. Barber, curate of Holy,Trinity, very pluckily seized the bridle and brought the animal to a standstill. The traffic was very heavy at the time,and a serious accident seemed imminent. The street was crowded with pedestrians, and the affair caused a goad deal of excitement. The rev, gentleman's conduct de- serves high commendation. We believe this is the second occasion on which he has been successful in stopping a runaway noro.

THE EXA MINER .

APRIL 213 t 1899.

JUST as the Greeks had to ba

The Cromwell reminded that there were heroes Tercentenary. before Agamemnon., it has become iaecessary to recall for tea instruction of an age .not too profound that there were great men of past centuries who contributed to the building up , of the British Constitution, the current faith, and the British Empire. This is often inconvenient and disturbing for shallow minds, prone to cere- Menial and floral tributes, and not unmoved by What THACKBRAY has assailed:as snobbery. The heroes of our country have not all been canonised as soon after their death as L3rd BEICONSFIELD. , Some of them were reviled as hypocrites, and for generations were the mirk of scorn and ridicule. CROIVW&LL was one of these mighty men. His as- sociates and contemporaries admired him, unless they suffered under his methods. But after his death a wave of obloquy passed over his name,,and the popular mind preferred to dwell upon the memory of CHA.RLE3 the Irtyr rather than of CR.OMWELL the Protector and the Puritan. Caom- WELL, in fact, had to be re-discovered, like buried statues. Roman pavements, and the cuneiform alphabet. When his private letters had been col- lected and commented upon it was found that he was a man in accord with the opinion his imme- diate friends had of him,and not the monster of the critics who came after hini,or who never forgave him for having routed a roystering aristocracy and driven an untruthful Menarch from the Throne. And now, three hundred years after his birth, we value him highly as a man of valour and military genius, as a ref orrner,a religious man,and a states- man. Hence it will not do to trust to the adula- tion or the defamation of the men who come just after the object they make the centre of their emotion. We remember that SERKESPEAME was not famous until long after he had passed away, and the fact makes us suspicious of fames and emblems that succeed the dead, like the recital of a herald at arms over the remains of a royal and raany-titled parson. It is most appropriate just now to remember that CROMWELL lived, and that he had a duty be- fore him which it was hard to fulfil, for on Tues- day occurs the tercentenary of his birth. Stern men are needed in rough tiraes,and his was a time When without the qualities he manifested there Would have been a deterioration of the national

character,a permanent enthralment of the national mind, possibly a political enslavement which might have changed the trend of our history for the worse. The KING was not a model of truthfulness and respect for popular rights. He was as false as a human being could be, and though a mistake was made when he was ex- cuted it was the only mistake in the campaign against him. The people of to- day would not stand a King who raised what troops he liked, and sent his messages to Parliament for what money he wanted, and levied taxes without the consent of Parliament, and imprisoned men without accusation or trial. The spirit which would resent these things is an inheritance fro in the CROMWELL period,and a strain of descent from the indomitable man himself. When we discuss such matters we Cromwellise without knowing it. We may say of the man himself what CLARENDON and HUME said,and what every Royalist scribe and Papal satirist has echoed everywhere, but the fact that we Cromwellise in our constitutional and political thinking is the surest, sweetest, and most convincing proof of his moral value in the history iof this country. To-day we permit no Sovereign I to hold an army or raise one independently of Par- liament, and taxes have to be levied by Parliament alone, and the Monarch is not superior to the law of the land. If there had been no CROMWELL, these things might not have come when they did, and might not be with us now to the extent which they are. He won for us our religious liberty, and put an end to the stupid absurdi- ties of Laud, that "cold, pedantic, ridiculous, superstituous " personage who tried to harry Puritanism out of existence and had Nonconfor- mist heretics branded with the letters S.S., as "Sowers of Sedition." He was as fair to the Jews as to the Nonconformists, and if he smote the Catholics of Ireland, it was not until they had made themselves conspicuous by ill-treating the Protestants. It is to CROMWELL WO owe that benign, sympathetic, liberty-promoting for eign policy which has done so much to make the name of England synonymous with freedom and relig- ious sincerity. He gave £2,000 out of his own pocket to help the Vaudois, "the slaughtered saints" for whose avengernent blind old MILTON prayed in one of his immortal sonnets. It would have been well for this country and the massacred Armenians if,in these "superior" days of enlighten- ment,we had been true to the noble ideal of CROM-. , NvELL's foreign policy, instead of swaggering about empire and then objecting to pay for the cost of it. An animating sense of public duty was one of CROMWELL'S characteristics. He was not a limp, boneless, scoffing kind of politician, seeking place and power, and careless of the laxity or the morality of the path-climbing, or the result. " I was by birth a gentleman," he once said," living neither in any considerable height nor yet in ob- scurity. I have been called to several employ- ments in the nation, and did ever discharge my duty as an honest man." "I have taken my oath to govern according to the laws which are now made; and I trust that I shall fully answer it," he said, on another occasion. "And know, I sought not this place." CROMWELL'S spirit is needed to-day. He was no Papist, and he would tolerate no mummeries. When he was Governor of Ely, a creature of Laim's, named Hirca, displeased him with his Papal antics in the cathedral. "Leave off your fooling and come down, sir" was CROMWELL'S re- buke—and the poor man came down. Up to Litres time the Communion Table had been in the middle of the church, and LAUD had it moved to the east end, where it is still allowed to re- main. Rails were put round, as CLAREN-DoN says, "Ito keep off the dogs." They are still to be found, and their origin may be worth remember- ing. The spirit of resistance to these innovations may be dubbed Puritanism, but it is as necessary to-day,as it was in CROMWELCS time. Yes,it is the consciousness that ceremonialism is killing the spirit of religion, that munameries are taken for faith, which gives to tercentenary views and feel- ings their keenness and depth. "Subtlety will deceive you," said CROMWELL, " integrity never will." The Protestantism of the day needs to be revivified and broadened with something of the CROMWELLIAN spirit, and reformers who may not desire to copy his rough methods will still remem- ber that revolutions are not won by fine phrases and hair-splitting, whilst liberty may be lost by submission to priestcraft,and prosperity imperilled by preferring private ease to public virtue.

THE problem of housing the Housing and Labour. working-class population is not in the least met by a device for enabling thrifty heads of families to buy their own houses. It would be a very simple [natter if it were so. The problem is of a two-fold character. There is the fluctuation of employment, now brisk in one place and anon in another, and there is the concentration of employment beyond the house room or the land-room of the locality. Labour must be mobile, and whatever would check the tendency must be bad for the providers of labour and for the labourers themselves. A conception of the problem to be solved which omits this mo- bility is of very little value. On the other hand the problem presented by a concentration of labour is even more painful, because it brings in its train the evils of high rents, over-crowding, and consequential poverty and disease. The two aspects of the case often present themselves simultaneously. Mobility engenders over-crowding, and it tends to produce high rents. A sudden influx of labour into a district leads at first to over-crowding, and then, if,the conditions are favourable, to rapid building and speculative business. If the district be already fairly well populated, the consequences are often very serious. Hence it is of no use facing the question as if it were simply one of providing houses for a settled and permanent industry, though that industry were liable to ups and downs. Certain general causes are at work behind the scenes which cemplicate every part of the entire question. There is the difficulty of getting suitable land for house building where the demand for houses is most imperative. Sometimes all the land is in the hands oE one owner, who will only let it on building leases. Glouncl-rents tend to become a very heavy infliction in many towns. High rents accompany all pressure, be it upon existing houses or upon newly-opened-out estates. Work men p-sy most exorbitant rents for very poor accom mo_ dation. How this will be cured by enabling thorn to buy their own houses is not eisy to see. The labourer who is here to-day and gone to-morrow will never buy his own house. He cannot afford it. High rents take se much from his earnings as to make saving for such a purpose impossible. Mobile labour increases the over-crowding pro- blem and defies the reformer who thinks owner- ship is the true solution of the difficulty. Buying his own house is an excellent thing for a man who is definitely attached to a

===== 0002601_18990421_art0050.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY APRIL 21, 1899. RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

settled industry distri But it has really no bearing on the general problem of how to house without over-crowding. In a slum dis- trict of any town, how - many heads of families could buy their own houses? How many good houses could be provided on the sites occupied by the slums ? Even when roomy houses are run up in the suburbs they are speedily found to be occupied by two families. In fact, the problem is bewilderingly complicated. The local authority seems to be the new power upon which we must fall back. But we must do it intelligently and comprehensively, and not ma muddle-headed way. The ground landlord cannot be 13ft to all his old powers. In fine, we must consider the entire problem before we can do anything effectively.

It must be confessed that the pessimistic fore- bodings in regard to the Workmen's Compensation Act have not been altogether borne out. This is a matter for rejoicing all round. The Parliamen- tary paper that has just been issued establishes conclusively the fact that the Act has been of immense benefit to the working classes, and that it has been loyally accepted by the masters. From the returns of the first quarter it is calculated that about 120,000 cases occur during the year,and out of these only 500 will be taken to the county court or the arbitrators. Solicitors' fees have been reduced to half the amount obtained under the Employers' Liability Act. And, moreover, the average compensation has risen from about £9O to £2OO. There is no reason to believe that the indus- tries affected by the Act cannot bear the burden. Some of the insurance companies are beginning to see that they can issue policies to the masters at cheaper rates. The Act was largely experimental, but the results so far are so cheering that no Government will be able to delay for a loni period an extension of the legislation to some of the trades that were so unfortunately, and in several instances unfairly, left out of Mr. Chamberlain's Bill. But the most satisfactory feature of the Act is that there should have been such a small pro- portion of litigation.

The habitual drunkard is a difficult person to deal with, but it was thought that the passing of the Inebriates Act would provide an easy solution to the difficalties continually cropping up. Un- fortunately, the Act is so far practically inopera- tive, for the very good reason that only three County Councils in the country have made any move towards fulfilling the duty imposed upon them of providing homes for habitual in- ebriates. At the .opening of the Liver- pool Quarter Sessions on Tuesday, Sir A. B. Forwood commented on the fact that most of the crime which came under his notice was due to the effects of strong drink, and he regretted the delay which had taken place in the establishment of reformatories for drunkards. As things at pre- sent stand, we are just where we were, and the drunkard who is perpetually in the dock can only be dealt with in the old ineffective way and sent back to gaol. Then there is the discomforting reflection that County Councils are deplorably slow-moving bodies in matters of this kind, and it is more than probable that two or three years , will have to pass before the intentions of the Act, 1 which has been law for some five or six months, are carried out to even a small extent. The Ine- briates Act is an excellent measure, and it is much to be regretted that the benefits which the toper and the punlic generally will experience, are so indefinitely postponed and so dependent upon the action of lethargic County Councils.

In another column we publish a letter from Mr. Alderman Roberts in reference to an article which appeared in the "Examiner" of a week ago on the subject of "Warrington and Secondary Education.' It was pointed out in the article that if the pro- posals of the Municipal Corporations Association in regard to Secondary Education were incorpora- ted in an Act of Parliament, Warrington would have no representative at all upon the local educa- tional authority, and would be entirely at the mercy of the Lancashire County Council. Those who are cognizant of the treatment which has been meted out to Warrington by the County Council in reference to Technical Instruction and the pro- posed widening of Warrington Bridge will have many misgivings if the influence of the County Council is extended to Secondary Education. Mr. Alderman Roberts characterises it as a "baneful influence," and strongly supports the proposal for the town to become a county borough at the earliest possible moment. This resolves itself mainly into a financial question, and before taking active steps it would be as well to know clearly what we are likely to gain and what we , shall lose by becoming a County Borough. The ' opposition of the County Council may be safely counted upon, as it is extremely unlikely that they will allow Warrington to slip from their grasp without a struggle. It is now some months since the Town Clerk was instructed to prepare a report on the question in all its aspects, but the work necessitated by the Corporation Bill and other important matters has delayed its completion. Such a report at this juncture would be both opportune and invaluable in guiding the Council as to what is the best course to take in the in- terests of the town.

Nothing could more surely point to the growth of Warrington and district than the steady rise in the value of assessments. During the past twelve months there has been an unprecedented increase in the rateable value of the Warrington tinion,ac- cording to the statement made by the Chairman of the Assessment Committee at the meeting of the Board of Guardians last week. At the commencement of the year the net rateable value ,of the Warrington Union. was £356,854 and it is now £379,290, or an increase of £22,406. The borough only forms a portion of the Union but its rateable value is considerably more than one half of the whole. At the present time the rateable ' value of the borough stands at £217,612 against that of £205,302 a year ago. It will therefore be seen that in Warrington alone there has been an increase of £12,310 in a single year, or roughly speaking, an increase of £l,OOO 1. month. This must be very gratifying. to all who are interested in the progress of \\ arrington. it is questionable whether any borough in the kingdom can show a like 'advance in rateable value. Few would care to predict that such an abnormal increase may be continuously looked forward to, but the outlook for Warrington as a great commercial centre is brighter than ever.

RUNCORN CHARACTER SKETCHES.

BY " Q.E.F.D."

Every inhabitant from the cynic to the gay,from the sage to the clown, will admit that Runcorn possesses a somewhat unique specimen of the bom- bastic type of individual. It is almost an insult to the intelligence of my readers to remind them there is a certain class ot men—limited enough I am happy te say or life would soon become unbear- able—who stint and fret their little span of exis- tence in the vain endeavour to ride the "high hors.e " over everyone. Naturally a stranger would not fail to note the urbane manner which is cul- tivated to a degree surprising and wonderful. Thera you have politeness personified if it is thought nothing can be lost and something may be gained by adopting such a manner. 'Tis a fatherly win- some smiling greeting that is extended all round. Who and what is be? Well neither Goliath nor Tom Thumb. He is not tall, neither is he short of stature. The first impression—which in this in- stance would be the worst—would doubtless be that he was a typical English squire: A short,well- set figure; benignant countenance and bland look heighten such an impression and partly convey the idea that he was a simple country man fresh from 80018 rustic village with a blissful ignorance of the ' busy city life. But this allusion is dispelled, as those who have had business dealings with this gentleman are aware. Indeed his shrewdness and far-sightedness are developed to an extent almost impossible to beat. Someone has said" It is a bad thing when a man knows he is clever !" It is quite true. Such per- sons will generally thrust their opinions most dis- agreeably before others because of this supposed ability. It is so in the present case. Undoubtedly he is the Sir Oracle of Runzorn society. When he opens his mouth, what right has anyone to chal- lenge the words of wisdom which fall therefrom. It will thus be gathered he is an übiquitous person, buoyed up with the idea that the cessation of his labours in the civic (if not the ecclesiastic) policy 'of the town would bring the whole to a sudden standstill. Well he has grounds far this opinion, and as a strong minded son of Runcorn deserves due honour. But one in loving him mast also love ' his peculiarities,and the greatest of Viese is " bom- basticism." Should you meet him in the street, the club, the football field, anywhere or even by acci- dent, you would know him. • BPIalt&X. Be man's station what it may. Some obtrusive display Of character. remains through thin an! thick. Yet of all the horrid traits; There's nothing that so grates As being " Bombastic."

RUNCORN NOTES,

[BY A COIiIIESPONDENT.]

The result of the election of Chairman to the Dis trict Council has come as a surprise to most pt.ople. No one is sorry or regrets nat Mr. Wiswall has been appointed; but the generality of the public were unaware that he was a candidate for the honour. Mr. Wiswall is highly respected and well-known, and is very popular with the working classes of the community, and we may feel certain of general and hearty support.—The vice-chairmanship was con- ferred upon the retiring chairman (dr. S. Taylor) according to a custom prevailing. Mr. T ,ylor is so well-known for his genuineness in all thing.; per- taining to the welfare of the town that his name has become practically a household word in Runcorn.

The Runcorn Free Church Council is afloat ! The inaugural meeting was held on Monday evening last at St. Paul's Weslel au Church, and the move- ment was well received—though general disap- Pointment was felt at the absence of Mr. Robert Veitch, M A., of Liverpool. The policy of the Free Church Council was clearly defined, and an assur- ance was given that it was not in any sense what- ever to be considered as a political organisation. This should be sufficient to give the lie to those who consider it an outcome of the late SchoDl Board Election. The success of the Council will remain in the hands of those Fres Churchmen who have the courage of their convictions and as they are many and enthusiastic there is a happy augury of some- thing tangible forthcoming. Toe solidifying of the interests ot the Free Churches in itself sufficiently merits of the Council's existenceowithout taking its poliey into consideration at all.

The action of the Gusrdians, in the matter of the rmfaal of the " Rising Sun Lodge of Odd- fellows," to pay contribution towards the mainten- ance of one of their members—as an inmate of the workhouse—has awakened a wide spread interest in Friendly Society's circles. Endeavours to em- body such a rule as that which has been so soundly abused by the Guardians, in other friendly societies are now on foot, and the ultimate result will doubt- less be that no friendly societies will be swelling the coffers of the Guardians at the end of another twelve months. Truly, "the stone has re- bounded."

At the meeting of the Halton Parish Council held last Wednesday evening,Mr Joseph Hannering was unanimously elected chairman, and Mr J Looker vice-chairman. The question of Astmoor Bridge came up for discussion and Mr Woodier (the clerk) read a letter received from Mr E Imison C C, stating he would take steps to bring the matter before the notice of those members of the County Council wha have the roads and bridges under their control. Considerable satisfaction was expressed at the reply.

A POLICEMAN'S OPINION OF THE GERMAN Em- PEROII.—An English gentleman was walking with a friend Unter den Linden. and in the course of a discussion on the Kair.,ei's conduct committed a grievous error of Majestats-Beleidigung. "The Emperor's a fool," he exclaimed, whereupon an English-speaking police officer tapped him on the shoulder, and said, "You must come mid me to ze police-station." "What for?" asked the Eng- lishman. "Mein herr did call ze Kaiser a fool," replied the man. No, no," urged the 'cute Briton, "It was the Russian Emperor I was talking about. "Dat will not wash," went on the constsble ; " dere is no Emperor a fool except the German Emperor." After which the police officer and the Englishman parted on good terms.

AN ARITHMETIC PROBLEM.—At a school ex- amination one of II.M. Inspectors was questioning a little boy in one of the lower standards, and found• that his knowledge of arithmetic was very deficient. The inspector had asked several ques- tions without getting a satisfactory answer, but, determined to arouse the little fellow's interest in the subject, he asked: "If your teacher gave you two rabbits, and I gave you one, how many would you have?" "Four, sir," replied the lad promptly. "Impossible,' replied the inspector, getting impatient. "Please, sir," said the little fellow, "I've got a lop-eared one already." AT a dinner one day the Duke of Wellington was talking with the dogmatic John Wilson Croker, who contradicted flatly about something which had occurred at Waterloo. The duke, knowing his man, submitted quietly; but not long afterwards Croker again became offensively asser- tive in regard to percussion-caps. "My dear Croker," said the duke, with unruffled good. humour, "I can yield to your superior informa- tion on most points, and you may perhaps know a great deal more of what passed at Waterloo than myself; but as a sportsman I will maintain my point about the percussion-caps!" A MEAN REVENGE. —He is paying cashier in ra bank, and he had words with the girl to whom he had been paying attention—so many words, in fact, that the girl, who is said to have more than a bit of temper, ordered him never to presume to speak to her again as long as he lived. She forbade him to recognise her, even, and said that she would make it a point to forget that she had ever met him. The other day she went, into the bank in which he is employed to have a cheque cashed. The blonde young man appeared at the window. He took the cheque .and examined it carefully. It was a broiling hot day. The girl was in a hurry. She hadn't an acquain- tance within half an hour's walk. The young man handed the cheque back. " Very sorry, madam," said he, "but you'll have to get some- body to identify. you bef.-Plk I can cash the theque."

The engagement of Sir Pater Carlavv Walker, Bart., to Miss Okeover is of more than ordinary in- terest. Sir Peter, who is the eldeit son of the late Sir Andrew Barclay Walker, who was wice Mayor of Liverpoll and fligh Sheriff of Lancaster, is a man of enormous wealth, in his fortv-sixth year. FINAL TIE.

GorztoasE V. BIIRTONWOOD.—The above teams met on Saturday on the Earlestown ground to decide who shall be the holders of the above trophy for the ensuing year. Soli] after half-past three the teams faced each other as follows :—Golborne : God, Ashton ; bscks. Smith and Houghton ; half backs, Cottam, Meekau, ant Skelhorn ; forwards, Sharratt, Chirton, Parr, Brogan, and Asiton. Burtonwood : Goal, Grice ; backs, Glennon and \loran; half backs, Doult)n, Murpny, and Peadis ; forwards, Hewitt, Bamber, Sullivan, Larkin, and Maddism.

Parr started on behalf of Burtonwood in the presence of about 500 spectators the majority of these having accompaniel their respective teams. The first item of importance came from Brogan and Ashton who carried the ball nicely up the right, but Glennon avertei the danger with a timely punt. Aided by the strong wind Burtonwood were soon hovering round Ashton again and Larkin eventually obtaining possession he Made n 9 mistake with a fine shot half-an-hour after the commencement. Roused by this reverse.the Golborners went off with a rush and an exciting melee took ploce in front of Grice, the custodian bringing off two or three marvellous saves whilst he was hampered by the Golborne forwards. A combined run by the Burton cod vanguard transferred the scene of op ,rations and Larkin scored their second point from a scriolmage in the goal mouth. Elalf-time, Burtonwood 2, Golborne nil. On resuming the Golborners soon began to assert their superiority and they gave the opposing defence an anx ous time. First Sherratt and then Parr trying their lack, the leather in each case being blown outside. From this point the Burtenwood team only crossed the half way line three or four times and then the Golloorne backs who all through the match played a splendid game, were too vigilant for them, and they never became really danger- ous. The Golborners were not long in opening their account. The right wing took the ball down and when well placed Brogan shot in. The custodian caught the ball but dropped it again and before he could regain possession he and the hall were bundlel into the net. The equalising goal came from the foot of Parr 15 minutes from the close. Final :—Golborno 2, Buttonwood 2. nil tie is to be replayed to-morrow (Saturday) on the Earlestown ground after the finish of the Alliance fixture between Earlestown and Parr. LANCASHIRE ALLIANCE.

RHODESIAN GOLD TRUST.

Interest during the past week has largely centred in the Rhodesian shhr3 market. The declaration of sub- stantiol dividends by three of the companies carrying on gold mining operations in Rhodesia an d the rise it- the price of Chartered shares to over £4.. have drawn con- siderable at:ention to everything Rhodesian. The lead- ing Berlin bankers are already largely interested in the success of many of the Ruode4an gold mines. It is said that Mr. Cecil Rhodes has had substantial assurances from the German Emperor and the English Colonial Office that his scheme for the continuation of the rail- way from Buluwayo northward, to connect with the direct line to Cairo, will be assisted by the guarantees ef the German and British Governments. Mr. Cecil Rhodes has said, however, that he is determined that the rail- way shall go on in any case. With cheap transportation thus assured in Rhodesia, there is every probability of the shares pf the Rhodesian Gold Trust going to a very considerable premium. The Rhodesian Gold Trust owns 2.157 claims in the best known and most important parts of Matabeleland and Rhodesia. There have been large dealings in these shares during the last account, and at the price of about Si per share, at which the £1 shares are no w dealt in on the London Stock Exchange, they are certainly worth the attention of the speculative in- vestor.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

- - - - - Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Eclitor. Slitters intended for publication must be written on one

side only of the paper. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has much greater chance of insertion than a long one.

MR. ALDERMAN ROI3ICRZ3 AND TEMPER-

Sir.—ln "The Examiner" of last Saturday you re- potted a speech on the Temperance question delivered bY Mr. Alderman Roberts on the preceding Wednesday at a Wycliffe Band of Hope meeting. I should be sorry to utter a wordolgainst that speech which bristled with facts and figures on the liquor traffic of an interesting and instructive character. Nor would I take any excep- tion to the meeting which called it forth. But having said this I must at the sime time plead guilty to a want of faith in the efficacy of Bands of Hope to stay the liquor plague, for one or two reasons. In the first place children have no natural propensity for strong drink; on the contrary lam inclined to think that most of them regard it as in abomination even worse than drugs. But the minds of the young are very plastic, and more easily operated upon far evil than for good. And then how very imita- tive they are, trying to copy their elders as far as pos- sible. When children are accustomed to see their friends and neighbours indulge in intoxicating liquors they naturally enough conclude that these liquors must have something good in them. And hence it comes to pass that as they grow older their aversion to strong drink becomes a diminishirg quantity until at length they take courage and began to follow the example set before them at home. Then again, when young people go out to labour in the great hives of industry, the lot of most them is cast among people who regard the public- bousss as right and proper places for amusement and entertainmen.t.l need hardly say that children and young persons so circumstanced at home and at work will hardly find the memories of Band of Hope meetings a power sufficient to save them from the manifold snares of the liquor dealers. Processions of nicely dressed little teetotallers are very pleasing spectacles to look upon, and would be infinitely more so if we would only believe that this young tem- perance life would be followed up by sobriety and steadiness in the years to come. But alas. sad °Aped- since does not permit one to indulge in such a pleasing dream. Childish shows and childish pledges, however attractive they may be, will never deliver this country from the liquor traffic and its terrible consequence!. Indeed, to quote front memory the words of an emi- nent brewer long since dead,"The struggle of the school, the church, and the library, against the gin palace and the beerhouse is only another development of the war between heaven and hell'. If we would save our children and future generations from the evils of these gin palace; and beerhouses we must destroy the trade of the brewers and publicans. There is no other way by which we can accomplish our object. And here the question arises, what are we doing in Warrington, to bring ttlie traffic to an end? Well, very little indeed. We hear now and then of temperance gatherings, and we had a spasmodic Sunday Closing meeting not long ago, but Ido not know of any thing else that is being done. On the other hand, however, the liquor traffickers and their friends are progressing. It seems as if the prosperity of the town was closely bound up with the making and selling of intoxicating drink, for the Carp3ration lately carried a scheme to provide unlimited water supplies for their best cus- tomers the brewees. Nor are the magistrates belping us in any way. The bench pay little or no ato tention to the oppeals that are made to them from time to time to curtail the facilities for drinking; but they are always willing and ready to bargain with th. brewer; in such a way as to ensure a greater turnover of drink.A.nd they seem to have altogether forgotten the flourish they made some two or three years ago of their Intention to do all they could to prevent the publicans from s.lling their wares to little coildren. At any rate nothing came out of that ietentian, and the traffic with the " little one; goes on as usual. Truly the prospect of temperance reform in Warrington is about as black as any putdican could desire it to 139.—Yours truly. lONA.

WARRINGTON AND SECONDARY EDUCATION.

Sir,—Your able article upon this important subject in your issue of Saturday last is well worth the perusal of every intelligent person. I need not say that I am in fullest sympathy with the proposal to conseitute War- rington a county borough. Our experience of the Lan- cashire County Council, upon almost every question, but notably in regard to technical instruction and the widening of Warrington Bri Igo, has been so unpleasant that I cannot conceive of any ram roae consenting to our being put to any greater extent under their baneful influence and control. If the proposals of the Municipal Corporations Association in regard to Secondary Education are incorporated in an act of Parliament, then unless in the meantime we be. Come a Ceneity Borough, are shall be divested of all con- trol la regard to one of the most, if not the most impor- tant quesnon of the future. Our continued progress and prosperity as a nation will be very largely dependent upon the. manner in which the Secondary Eincation of the rising generation is treatel.Myour article very justly states our hands have been tied in Technical Instruction matters by the niggardly and most unjuut doles which it has pleased the a.rrogance of the County Couneil to bestow upon us. Persoaally I have a roeted obj te receive anything in the shape of a " greet," to whieh I have a moral or legal right. We are being annually deprived of a serious proportion of the full amount of money which the allocation of the heir tax for Techni- cal Instruction purposes entitles us to receive. The re• suit is that our operations are crippled for wan: of funds, and our town's children are defrauded of their lawful inheritance.

Surely after this experience we are not prepared to eurrendar our liberty of action and the control of our own affairs into the hands of an authority which has already served Us so scurvily. If we are Emperor its this matter and (from the fear of the possibility of a temporary increase of expenditure which cannot amount to much and may even not occur at all) allow ourselves to lose the opportumity of becom- ing a County Borough and consequently the educational authority for our own district, then indeed shall we deserve " kicks" instead of the half-pence which we shall most certainly and deservedly get from the Lanca- shire County Council. Let our motto be" Warrington a County Borough." Yours faithfully,

H. ROBERTS.
