RUNCORN FREE CHURCH COUNCIL. INAUGURAL MEETING AT ST. PAUL'S.

There was a large and representative attendance gathered in St. Paul's Wesleyan Church, on Monday evening, at the inaugural meeting of the Runcorn Free Church Council, an institution whicla has been formed in the town with the following cemmenriable objects : "(1) To cultivate closer friendship between the Evan- gel al Frae Churches; (2) to undertake such united Evangelistic Work as may be deemed practicable; (3) to promote the law of Christ in every relatioa of human Life, and. to assist in all measures for the advancement of public morality; (4) to provide opportunities for the exposition and enforcement of Free Churcti principles, and safeguard the rights of the associated Nonconfor- mist Churches." The chair was occupied by the Rev. C. W. 1.. Christie' (president of the Council) who was supported on the platform by Mr. Percy W. Bunting, M.A.. (editor of the "Comtemportry Review," London), Revs W Fidoe, S Wright, Syd. T Morris, R J Clifton, J 03bor e. B A. Messrs F J Nermen, James Pritchard, W Handley, T Simister, R H Pomatt, and others. The Rev. J.; Osborne asked those present to remember one or two things in order that they might see the Free Church Council had not sprung up from any sense of hostility or in antagonism to the Established Church. It had come very gradually. It had not sprung up ia a moment of enthusiasm. Clearly the Free Church Coun- cil had sprung up naturally from a clear sense of duty, and atter prolonged discussion of the whole subject. By a Curious coincidence all the .None infermist ministers of the town with the exception of the Rev. W. Fidoe settled in Runcorn about the same time, somewhere between June and Saptember, 1897. Vary naturally they cello i utoon one another to exchange congratul tarsus upon the circumstances that Providence had favoured them with so nice a little town as Runcorn. In a very shert time one or two of them suggested that it would be a good thing if a monthly Minister's Fraternal were formed as a means of strengthening their personal interest in each other, and in the work of their respective churches. As the remilt of that suggestion the ministers met in December 1897, and it was at that meeting that the idea of a Free Church Council was first discussed. Beene new to the Town and not knowing the relation of the Free IChurches to one another, and to the other churches o the town, they resolved to wait until oppertueity had been given to them to ascertain the feeling of the Ica line laymen of the various Free Cnurches, and contented themselves with the formation of a Ministers' Fraternal That waq over 16 months ago. Shortly after a lette• was received from the Rey. R. Veiteh, M. A..,Secretary to the Liverpool and District Federation of Evangelistic Free Churches asking if steps could betaken in the direc- tion of forming a Free Church Council. The letter was re- ferr,d to the Ministere'Fraternal,but no further action was taken at the time. As time went 03 their knewledem of the situation increased, ant having counselled with pro- minent laymen in the Free Churches, the conviction was borne in upon them that steps should be takin to form an Evangelical Free Church Council. The Miaisterse Fraternal cage I a meeting of laymen belonging to all the Evangelical Free Churches of the town, and after consi- derable discussion, it was decided to invite the Rev. R. Veitcb, of Liverpool, to corns over and explain the aims and advantages of the Free Church Council newer:lent. Mr Veitch clime over and gave tn exposition so luminous and so deeply pregnant with Caristien spirit that a re- i solution in favour of a urea Church Council for Runcorn was carried unanimously. It was also decided at that meeting that the Ministers' Fraternal should draw up a constitution for the Council, to be submitted to another: meeting of Free Church representatives for criticism andl ultimate adoption. A meeting for that purpose was called early in January of this year, and after one or two minor alteratioos the constitution was unanimously adopted. Here Mr. Osborne quoted two or three para- graphs from the constitution, setting forth the objects of the Council and the principle of membership. Ir was then left with the various Free Churches to decide whether they wou!d join the Council, and if they decided to do S 3 to elect their representatives forth- with. The five Free Churches, through their official committees, heartily associated themselves with the Council, and the first meeting of duly elec ed represen- tatives was held in Camden Schoolroom on January 29 h. At pre sent the Council consisted of 60 representatives and five personal member,. The offilials for the first year were elected as follows :—Rev. C. W. L. Caristien president, Messrs. W. Handley and John Wilsen vice presidents, Mr. R. FI Posnett treasurer with the Rev. J. Osborne and Mr. Weeley C. Cooke as secretaries. That was the history of their Council so far. It now remained for all of them to make its history in the future, whim. he trusted, would be a noble history—(epplause)— worthy of these churches of theirs, but, most of all, worthy of that religion which they loved an I which they existed to maintain. (Applause.) He added just a word to the younger Nen:conformists—amongst whom he reckowd hirreelf. 'Fury should have in the Council a strength and an inspiration they had never felt In days when it was easy Co be disloyal to the C lurch into which thee had been born they should take he art— (applause)—tne they should have more nerve in stand- inc ue for the grand traditioes which were the eery of the Free Churehes. (Applause.) The C erten was com- posed of all the ministera and representatives who had been e'ectel by the Free Churches ie the folio wine pro- pertisn :—Wasleyans, 20 members ; Congregational.nine members ; Primitiv Methodist Church, nine membees ; Presbyterians, nine members ; and the Crated Metho- dist Free Onurch, sit memoere. (Applause.) The Rev.C.W.ls Christian, who was well received said their position that night was that they were gatheree as representatives of the Evangelical Free Churches. They represereed Churches that differed but in the matter of Goeernment and discipline. No one in his senses would suppose that one system of Church Govern- ment would suit every class of human mind, and every stage of human development in all c iMee an I genera- tions. When they came to the New Testament they did not find that God had laid down any hard and fast system of church governmeut. If he might be allowed the expres-ien, G had lift Himself free to guide His people according to the exigencies of the times in which they might live. But they were one in those fundamen- tal doctrines which they held fast to the death. (Ap- plause.) Distinct as the billows, they were one as the sea. They were Evangelical Churches. Taey held fast the doctrine Qf their holy religion which they associated rightly with the divinity .f their Lord Jesus Cadet. They held the atoning sacrifice that He off--herd on the cross for human redemption. They held the doctrine of justification by faith, and sanctificstion by the Spirit. They held that the Bible was their rule of faith arid Prictice, and they federated themselves because they believed they would be more powerful in their generation by their union than they would be by being disionitsd. (Applause.) They stool forth before the werli that night as Protestants, as patriots, and as oml:relying they believed, the best elements of the beet Puritanism. (Ap- plause.) Tney were Protestants, ani, therefore, in antogonisrn to the Church of Rune. T iev loc,ed Romans, but they detested Romanising praetic is. They denied all the great and peculiar doernat of that system,includ- ine baptismal cos:aura:ion, the mass, the invoc sties of saint'. Mariolity, absolution, purgatory, prayers for the dead, the witholding of the Bible from the people, the spirit of persecution whets had been the very breath of tee nOStriei of that system for lone ages. They objected equally to the Rosnauising portion of the Established Mame of this country. (Applause.) Don't let them be misunderstood. They h ad the most powerful and tender Sympathy with the Evangelical portion 07 the Established Cheroh. (Applause) They knew many of tie noble ra en who were preaching the pure gospel within the pale of the Established Church. Tory knew how God was bleating their labours, and they thanked God for that blessing. Who among them that ni !fit did not say God bless Bisbee, Ryle ? (Applause). Would that all his brethren on the Bench were as teu3 to the Bible as Behop Rye,. (Applause). The speaker went on to enumerate other clergy and laymen in the Church oi Egamd, who, he said, were doing a noble work. They rejoiced in the centenary of the Church Missionary Secisey, which was being held in Len ton at the present time. That was an Evangelical branch of the Church of Christ, and 300 ordained missionaries from this country were labouring abroad in connection with that society to-layeand neatly 300 native ordained ministers had been brought to Christ through their instrumentslity. The soasty was work- hog in all the foreign missionary fields in beautiful bar- mouy with the missionaries of the Free Churches. (Ap- plause.) Every young man who was cony: rted to tied and who felt called to preach the pure doctrine of the Lord Jesus Christ was a gain to the Established Church. But they did object to the Remanising, portion of the Established Church. They said every man who was eating the bread of a Pro- testant Church and teaching Popish doctrine was are act- ing lie. If he (the Chairman) no longer believedin Me'hodist doctrine, and yet stayed in the Meth) list Church, if arch a possibility could occur, he should feel that he was a tale man through and through. Taey said to every society which taught Popery within the Protestant Church, which existed to protest against Popery—they said "Ye are wolves in see'p's clothing ; resign your livings and go your ways that we may have the privilege of thinking of you as honest men." (Applause.) As , Protestants and peerhrs they obj cted to Popery, and they objsctedto the unmasked romanising, in the Establiseted Church. They were the representatives of Churches which Gad had wonderfully blessed in the pest. How many souls had been saved from sin by the miniery of the Free Churches,only God knew. The Free Churchts had been in the van of all progress. They had been in the van of social ant political liberty. They had been on the side of education for the masses of the people, for theiredress of wrongs, for the removal of unjust monopolies, or the giving to every ctizen a fair chance in the race of life, and they were the relentless foss of all those evil forces

THE EXAMINER, FRIDAY, AP.RH 21. 1399. LOCAL WEDDINGS. 'NEWTON TTRBAN DISTRICT

which threatened their country. They had what was celled a Nonconforrniet conscience, and it was better to have a Nonconformist conscience, than no eonscience at all. (Applause.) They had a conseiencs that made them the enemies of the drink traffic, the enemies of im- morality, of betting, and gambling, the enemies of everything that could degrade England. They were standing against the Sunday issues of daily papers. (Loud applause.) If they would contrast the state of England to-day with what it was in other times, they might set it down as an historical fact that the Free Churches had had the lion's share in the glorious change that had been brought about. (Applause.) Mr. Percy W. Bunting, who received quite an ovation said they were assembled to uphold a movement for the union of the &range:Leal Free Churohes, and the first question to consider was how these churches ever be- came disunited. All were churches of Christ, and all were founded on very much the same conception of the Gospel of Christ. They were all Evangelical, although they were different in the way in which they managed their affairs. There used to be differences in doctrine. Still it seamed at first marvellous that bodies of Christians having the same view of religion of the New Testament, holding the same Saviour, having the same Idea exactly of Christianity, which depended upon the personal faith of the members, believing that the same Spirit in the same way actuated the Church—it seemed rem trkable that they should be acting quite separately on the seine grounds. Hare in Runcorn they had five Evangelical Free Churches, and they had to go into the very minutiae of Church life in order to find out what the differences were. How did this happen? In the lech century there was a great religious revolt against the authority of the Ceurch of Rome. At that time there were no Free Churches or vary few. The whole Christian world proceeded upon the idea that there was one Christian Church and one only. Whoa the Church had authority it declared what was Christian truth and what ought to be Obristian life, The Church of Rome became sunk in superstition, more and more tyrannical and bloodthirsty in its government, and the way in which it repressed all differences in opinion, and all active thought, was to put them down with the strong hand of lire and sword. Every view of the Gespel which differed from its own ; every whisper which had not the author- ity of the Church of Reins was repressed by phyeical means. The revolt against the Church was by those who were scandalized at the corruption and sins of Rome. It was at a tims when the Popes were unauestionably wicked men, sunk in vice and cerruption and profligacy of the worst kind, when they were murderers and robbers' lascivious men and peisoaere, Tnese ware men who ruled in the name of Jesus, and the revolt was against this whole system of wieked and worldly govern- ment. There was no alternative church to appeal to, and in a revolt against all that authority that existed they set up the right of every man to live up to his own conscience. The result was a return to Primitive Chris- tianity, and the reformers searched the New Testament in order to find a definite scheme of Government by which the Church was to be manaeed, but they could no; fled one. They fund a great deal about jostificition by faith, the election of persons to menage congregations, and the apostles going about confirming riders and deacons. The consequence was that people took different views. The speaker went on to describe the origin of the Baptists, Presbyterians, and Congregationalists, and later the Wesleyan Methodisss,which were as much a reformation enure) as those previ outly named. There were use in those times doctrinal difforencem.but within recent years these seemed to have gone. If they went into four dif- ferent evangelical churches to-day they would hear the same sermon in them all. Teeny preached the same doc- trine, and they carried on their churches very much in the same style, and the National Free Church Ceuncil movement had sprang up under their feet because the churches all over the country were abselutety ripe for it. It had moved with such rapidity that those who initiated the movement had had to follow and not to I lead. What were they to do? They were to hold up the ereat standard of evangelical truth in a country which seemed disposed to run away from it. Multitudes of the hest people in the Church of England were shocked and horrified at what they saw going around under a romaaising clergy. The other day, in spite of the anxiety and the skill of one of the most ekilful politicians, the plain common sense of the members of the House of Commons bade him be- ware, and he was told by Churchmen bound by party allegiance that the nation was not going to be led back to Rome, and that the clergy must obey the law or go. (Applause.) God send them strength to abide by that declaration. (Hear, hear.) Ha had no hostility to the Church of England at all. The one teiag the Church of England needed at this moment was a schism. Schism had been, and perhaps might be again, the method by wh oh God taught the truth t o them. Schism was better than compromise when the Gospel truth was in danger. In face of the danger to which the National Church was exposed it was the poeitien of the National Free Church Council to maintain an alternative Church. The Free Caurches w ore now federated and organize I to. feather in one National C uncil, which in itself was a National 'Cnurch according to all the definitions of a church. They held up this alternative church before tlie masses of the people of this country. The Frei Church Council would have to attack public immerality.nerruet mueicipilile I, the suppression of the dienk trafic and gambling and vice, and hold up a Caristian Church destined and determined to maintain a high standard of morals. (Applause.) Mr. Bunting spoke in conietnna • tion of the priestly Clailla9 of ties Roman Church. and said the democracy was th o soul of a true Christian Church. In the present ritualistic crisis the question lay between Christianity and the priesthood, and if the Evangelical party in the Church of Englaed would shill upon the truth of the Lord Jesus Christ, and rely upon the support of the Nonconformists, they were bound to win. (Applause.) The Chairman announced that from seine unexplained Cause, the Rev Robert Voitch, of Liverpool. bad not put in an appearance. Mr. R. H. Posnett proposed the following resolution;— "That tnis meeting hails will hearty approval the for- mation of an Evangelical Free Church Council in Run- corn, and calls up m all Nonconformists in the town an .l district to support it loyally and enthusiastically." He took it that the resolution really meant Vat they should become more brotherly and more fraternal than they had been before. He understood it to mean that they should avoid in their villages that great over-lappiee which had been one of the great troubles which. their Free (.3teurehes had had to meet with all throuele the pre_ seat century. One had not to go further than Main street, Feodsham, to see the over-lapping of Nonconfor- mist Churches. They would see there more churches for Nonconformists than tnere were in the whole of Fred- sham and Overton. He bed been brought up and nursed in the lap of Nonconformity, and he did not agree with one remark of the Chairman. He did not imagine any- one who had been nursed and brought up in Nonconfor- mity going to the Church. He sometimes tried ti imagine himself really going, but he did not know what thee would do with him. He did not think he would make a Churchman. He thought a great deal of the to- formation was due to the leading they got from Oliver Cromwell. He taught them and he believed they were learning the lesson, that all liberties were sealed and bound up by their preserving the liberty of conscience. Mr. Frit :nerd secended, aud toe motion was carried unanimously. Votes of thanks to the chairman, choir, &c., brought the meeting to a close.

I On Monday afternoon Mr. S. Brighouse held an in- quest at S. Helees Tewn Hall on the body of Jams Johnson, 42 sears of are, labourer, of Marshall's Cross- road, Sutton. who died in the Providence Hospital on ISaturday from the effeets of injuries received at Har s'utw Brook Chemical Works on the previous day. Mr. Lauder, ass•stant inspector of fantories and worksh was present ; Mr. R. W. H. Thomas appeared for Mr William Taylor, contractor, by whom deceased had been employed; and Mr. H. L. Riley represented the relatives of the deceased man.—William Taylor, contractor, Helena-road, said that on Friday afternoon they were hoisting a beam of wood, 24ft lone' and weighing abut 4cwt. Deceased was in the act of guiding one end of the beam when a link of the chain broke and deceased {AI, sustaining the injuries from which he died.—The Coroner, after examining the broken link,said there was evidently a flaw in it. —Witness said it was a very slight flaw,and was not noticed until it broke.—The Coroner : How long have you had this chain in use ?—Witness.: About two years.—The Coroner: Had you this chain tes'ed from time to time ?—Witneee: I have not teatel it there.—The Coroner : My experience is that they go ma working chains nntil they break. —Witness said they did not do that. If they ever saw a flaw and found the chain faults, they did not use it.—Jaints Monsey, in the employ of Mr. Taylor, said he examined the chain, ropes, and hoisting arrangements at the works about ten days ago. Everything then appeared perfectly safe. He examined each link of the chain, and did not notice n y fie w.—Tlte jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." LIBEEtAL MEETING AT EARLESTOWN'

A meeting of Liberals was held in the Primitive Methodist schoolroom, Eerlestewn on WednesJay even- ing, for the purpose of hearing a lecture by Mr. a. B. Sh)ree of Widnes, on "Three years of Conservatism—A contr tet." This was one of a series which has b ,en given dur ug the past week or two in vari )us parts of the Diviiiion. Mr. E. J. Evans presided. The lecture which was illustrated by lime light views was of an interesting character and set forth the work of the Conservative party during their term of office, as contrasted with the work of the Liberal party, the illustrations being ex- ceptionally good. At the close Mr. Shores said it had been found in all the groat movements of the time th it orga.nieation was indispensable, and in order to deal most effectively with their opponents. they must, as they did, organise. The Tories of the Newton Division, he believed, were ex- tremely well organised, and he tho eght it was import- ant, that they as Lib,rels shoeld bs in a similar state. There was a system which he thought they in the past had been in the habit of following, it was that they should form a Liberal three-hundred, and ' also that they should elect an executive. They found in the Newton Division that it had been the peaetice te anpoint 62 persons to represent the Polling Districts of Newton and Earlestown, and they should chose but of that number a certain number to act upon the executive which had to control the organisation, lectures, distri- bation of literature, and the selection of a candidato at an election. The other committee was a body of Liberals associated for the propagation of Liberal principles, a body which he hoped would be constantly on the alert for the advancement of Liberalism for the particular polling district with which it was connected. tie sug• yestad that they should receive names of all who might be useful on the committees and if they knew of any active Libera's, they might mention their names, and they could be communicated with to accept nomination. After apoeinting the 62, they could select from their number 10, who according to the list, they thought should represent them on the Executive Council. Several names were than taken of those willing to act on the committee.

Mr. Gilmour said he would gladly give his name as a manaher or the committee, but he had to decline to do so that might, for one or tw3 reasons. One was that he had not time to devote to it, and the other was that he could mot at the last rnorrielt go in and support a candi- date who villuld not nurse the Division.

Mr G W Rawlins said the reason they did not get a candidate at the bye-eleation was because they had no organisation. The attitude of Mr Gilmour and gentle- men of his mind would perpetuate that condition, and they must organise themselves before they could get a candidate. At the last general election many worked very hard, and he (himself) did all he could to get a candiJate to come forward. They had every pessible assistance from Mr. Ellis, and other Liberal leaders, and every candidate who was pressed to CODas forward, said their organisation ha fallen to pieces, but if it was in good working order, they would he very pleased te come )rward and fight for them. Uneil the organisation was got into a working condition there WAS no possibility of getting any candidate If they organised themselves and took that opportuue time which now presented itself, they would before long get a candidate, and when they did he would be ()le selected for the purpose of nursing the constituency. T.ies3 meetings which were being held all over the constituency wire the outcome of Mr. Ellis' labours, as he wished to do eis best to asst tee Newton Division, and he (Mr. Rawlins), regretted he was not now alive. He did not give up hope in the Newton avision, and he, the sneaker, hoped they were going to justify the faith Mr. Ellie had in them. Earlestown contained a good number of Radicals, and they only wanted banding togetber. They must organise them- selves for the cmning reeistraeion, they must have such a thorough regicration this year, as that constituency bad never before been seen.

A. number of hame s were then motioned as likely to blcome membsrs of the committee. A vote of thanks to the Chsirman and Mr. Shores, closed the proceedings.

THE ASSISTANT OVERSEER

Mr. Clarke, in moving an increase in the salary of the. assistant overseer, said that wnen the parishes of Stock- ton Heath and Appleton were joined, with regard to the overseership the asseesimmta were £B6O, but when they were divided the figurel were—Stockton Heath £579, Anpletom £2Bl. With regard to the demand notes, 300 had to be sent out for Stockeon Heath. 160 for Appleton, :end 170 for Weton Inferior. When Mr. Ellison had the two parishes he was paid £5O a year for a.llecting the poor rate, and when the work was divided, although Mr. ISradihaw had more than twice the amount of work to do, he was only voted half of the a!ary, namely £25. Toe assistant overseer for Walton Inferior, althowgh his demand notes were only ecria,l to the App,eton demand notes in number, was receiving £25 for the ccllvction. Since Mr. Bradala,.w commenced the collection of the rates from the 25th March, 1898, to the 25th March of this year, the increase in the assessment amounted to about £4•88 He end )rsed every word which had been said about the manner in which the rate books were kept and the rate collector, end he proposed that the minute ef the 22nd February, 1898, be rescinded, and that Mr. Bradshaw be reappointed assistant overseer at a salary of £3O.

Mr kason seconded, and the motion was carried. The other business was of a formal character.

CONGREGATIONAL CHURCH, NEWTON.

_ The anniversary sermons in aid of the schools connected with the above church were preached in the church on Sunday. The day being fine there were large congregations at each service. Appropriate sermons were preached morning and evening by Professor E. Armitsge. M.A., of Bradford, his dis- courses being listened to with intense interest. Special hymns and anthems were sung by the choir, Mr. A. Knight presiding at the organ. In the after- noon, a musical service was given in the school- room, before a crowded audience, when the follow- ing programme was submitted :—Anthem, "I will sing of Thy power ;" solo, "Cathedral voices," Miss Mather; quartette," But the Lord is mindful; se- lection, "Onward, ever onward," by the children; solo and chorus, "0 take my hand," Miss Nellie Mather and choir ; solo," Arm, arm, ye brave," Mr. Knowles; selection, " Springtime," children ; duet, "0 praise the Lord," Miss Mather and Mr. White ; chorus," The heavens are telling," choir; quartette, " Blessed are the merciful ;" solo, " Creations hymn," Miss Mather; selection, "Lift the Gospel Banner," children and choir. The collections during the day amounted to £27 -

A NEWTON YOUTH'S ATTEMPTED SUICIDE.

On Wednesday marning, at the W arrington. County Petty Sessions, Geirge Frederick Archer 20 years of age, residing at 22, Wargrave-road Newton, was charged with attempting to commit suicide at home on the 4th April, by cutting his throat with a razor.—Elizabeth Harrison said she was defendant's sister, and he resided with her. A few days previous to the 4th April, defendant was very ill. He went to bed at 8 p.m. on Monday, the 3rd April, and witness subsequently found him lying across the bad with his clothes on. She asked him what he was doing like that, and he replied that his head was bad. She told him to go down- stairs for a powder. He did S), and afterwards went to bed. He Was vomiting an: coughing all night. The nest morning her son s!arted screaming and ha told her that defendant had cut his throat. Wit- ness sent for a doctor and the police. Defendant was troubled with fits.—Yaornas Hirrison, defen- dant's nephew, said that on April 3rd he slept in the same room as defendant. On the following morning defendant wakened him, and told him to go and tell his mother that he- had cut his throat. Witness did so and afterwards went for a doctor.— P.C. Schofield stated that he found the defendant at seven a.m. on the 4th met, sitting in the kitchen with his throat cut. He found the rP.zor produced covered with blood on the table. Defendant was ordered to be talc,ifi to Warrington Infirmary.—ln- spector Morgan rernarkei that defendant promised to take care of himself in the future. Toe wound 11 was not properly healed yet. On promisinQ to be- have himself better in the future defendant was discharged.

MILL FIRE AT OLDIII3I. A fire broke out at abaut One o'clock yesterday after- noon in the top spinning roans of the Alma cotter' milt of Messrs. P. Schofield and Co., Limited, Ashton-road, Oldham. The 011harn, Werneth, and Tewnfield bri- gades harried to the scene and found the flames raeiag fiercely in the upper storey of aJarge block. *The flames made their way well into the fourth storey, and I after about two hours and a half the fire was practi- cally setedued. Damage amounting to £3,000 had then been done. ALL-NIUHT SITTING OF A DISTRICT COUNCIL. The Harwich Dictict Ceuneil concluded a sitting of over 12 hours at 8.30 yesterday morning. The Council net the previous night to elect a chairman. The Liberals proposed Mr James Doagill, centractor, and the Censer- vativee Mr John Fletcher. an Independent gentleman. The voting was equal. Neither side would give way, and refresnments were seat for. The members lighted their pipes and celled for newepspers. At six this morning the Conservatives moved an adjeernenent, and left the meeting in a body. The Liberals afterwards appointed Mr Deugill as chairman, and the meeting was adjourned until Monday.
